  Now, let's dig into your idea. I'll ask questions one at a time to refine

  ---
  First question: What's the primary interaction model?

  When LLMs participate in a conversation, how do you see the dynamic working?
  Here are a few options:

  A) Council/Debate mode — The user poses a question/topic, and multiple LLMs
  discuss it among themselves while the user mostly watches. The user can jump
  in to steer or ask follow-ups, but the primary flow is LLM-to-LLM discussion.
  Think of it like a panel debate with the user as moderator.

  B) Group chat mode — The user and multiple LLMs are equal participants in an
  ongoing conversation. Anyone can "@" anyone. The user chats with LLMs like
  normal, and LLMs can also respond to or build on each other's messages. Like a
   Slack/Discord channel with AI participants.

  C) Orchestrated agent mode — The user gives a task, and LLMs collaborate to
  solve it (one researches, one critiques, one synthesizes). The user sees the
  internal back-and-forth but it's framed as a workflow rather than casual chat.
   More like watching a team of agents work through a problem.

  Which of these feels closest to what you have in mind? (Or is it something
  else entirely?)

● Got it — group chat mode. User and LLMs as equal participants, anyone can
  respond to anyone.

  Second question: What triggers an LLM to respond?

  In a group chat with multiple AI participants, you need rules about when they
  speak — otherwise every message could trigger a flood of responses from all
  LLMs. How should this work?

  A) @mention only — An LLM only responds when explicitly @mentioned by the user
   or another LLM. Full control, no unsolicited responses. Feels intentional but
   requires active management.

  B) Auto-response with configurable participation — LLMs respond based on
  configurable triggers: always respond, respond only when @mentioned, or
  respond probabilistically (e.g., "chime in 30% of the time when the topic
  matches your expertise"). Gives each LLM a "personality" for how chatty it is.

  C) Round-based / Turn-based — The conversation proceeds in rounds. Each round,
   every participating LLM gets one chance to respond (sequentially or in
  parallel). The round ends when all have spoken or passed. Clean structure but
  more formal.

  D) Hybrid — Default is @mention, but the user can toggle any LLM into
  "auto-participate" mode where it freely responds like any chat participant
  would.

  Which direction feels right?

  Third question: How do LLMs connect to the app? Do users bring their own API 
  keys?

  A) BYO keys — Users configure their own API keys (OpenAI, Anthropic, Google,
  etc.) for each LLM participant. Full flexibility — users pick any model from
  any provider, pay their own API costs. The app handles no billing.

  B) Built-in providers + BYO — The app ships with some free/built-in models,
  and users can optionally add their own keys for premium models.

  C) Built-in only — The app manages API access behind the scenes. Users just
  pick from available LLMs. Simpler UX but means you'd handle API costs and
  provider relationships.

  Which model makes more sense for what you're building?

● Simple and clean — no billing to worry about.

  Fourth question: What's the deployment model?

  Since users bring their own keys and the app is web-based, there are a few
  ways to deliver it:

  A) Pure client-side SPA — A static web app that runs entirely in the browser.
  API keys stored in localStorage, calls made directly from the browser to LLM
  providers. Simplest to build and deploy (just static hosting), but some LLM
  providers block browser CORS requests, and API keys in the browser are a
  security concern.

  B) SPA + thin backend proxy — The frontend is a web app, but there's a
  lightweight backend that proxies LLM API calls. Keys still stored client-side
  or in a simple session. Avoids CORS issues, adds minimal complexity. Can be
  self-hosted easily.

  C) Full-stack web app with backend — Backend manages conversations, message
  history, LLM routing, and key storage (encrypted). More complex to build and
  deploy, but more secure and enables server-side features like conversation
  persistence across devices.

  Which direction are you thinking?

  Fifth question: What's your tech stack preference?

  This is a greenfield project, so you get to pick. A few natural fits for an
  SPA + thin backend:

  A) Next.js — React-based, handles both the SPA frontend and the thin API proxy
   in one project. Vercel deployment is trivial. Huge ecosystem. Good if you
  want to optionally grow into more server-side features later.

  B) Vite + Express/Fastify — Vite-powered React (or Vue/Svelte) frontend + a
  minimal Node.js backend. Clean separation between frontend and backend. Very
  flexible.

  C) Python FastAPI + vanilla JS/HTMX — If you prefer Python for the backend
  proxy. Less SPA-like but simpler if that's your primary language.

  D) Something else? — Let me know if you have a preference.

  Sixth question: How should messages appear — streaming or all-at-once?

  A) Real-time streaming — LLM responses stream token-by-token as they're
  generated (like ChatGPT). This means WebSockets or SSE. HTMX handles this fine
   with SSE extensions, but it adds backend complexity since you need to manage
  concurrent streaming connections for multiple LLMs that might be responding
  simultaneously.

  B) Full message delivery — LLM generates the complete response, then it
  appears as a single message. Simpler to implement (HTMX polling or a simple
  refresh), but feels less interactive. The user stares at a "thinking..."
  indicator until each LLM finishes.

  C) Hybrid — Stream the LLM that the user directly interacts with, but
  non-urgent auto-responses from other LLMs appear as complete messages.
  Balances UX with complexity.

● Good — responsive where it matters, simple where it doesn't.

  Seventh question: What do LLMs "see" of the conversation?

  When an LLM generates a response, what context does it get?

  A) Full conversation history — Every LLM sees every message (user + all other
  LLMs). Most natural for group chat — they have full context and can reference
  anything. But context windows fill up fast with multiple verbose LLMs, and
  you'll need to manage token limits.

  B) Directed messages only — An LLM only sees messages where it was @mentioned,
   plus the message it's replying to. Efficient but limits spontaneous insight —
   an LLM can't build on what another LLM said unless explicitly included.

  C) Sliding window + @mentions always — Each LLM sees the last N messages from
  the full conversation, plus all messages where it was @mentioned (regardless
  of distance). Balances context awareness with token management. N could be
  configurable per LLM.

● Architectural Approaches

  Here's a summary of what we've locked in before I propose approaches:

  ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │     Decision     │                        Choice                        │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Interaction      │ Group chat — user + LLMs as equals                   │
  │ model            │                                                      │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ LLM              │ Configurable: @mention, always-on, or probabilistic  │
  │ participation    │                                                      │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ LLM connectivity │ User brings own API keys                             │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Deployment model │ SPA + thin backend proxy                             │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Tech stack       │ Python FastAPI + HTMX                                │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Message delivery │ Hybrid — streaming for directs, full-message for     │
  │                  │ auto                                                 │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ LLM context      │ Full conversation history per LLM                    │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Persistence      │ Yes, across sessions                                 │
  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Users            │ Single-user, no auth, SQLite                         │
  └──────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ---
  Approach A: Async FastAPI + SSE Streaming (RECOMMENDED)

  A single FastAPI process using Python's asyncio to handle everything. User
  messages trigger async LLM generation tasks. Direct interactions stream via
  SSE; auto-responses run concurrently via asyncio.gather. HTMX renders
  templates, with SSE extension for streaming chunks.

  Pros: Single process, no extra infrastructure (no Redis, no workers). Async is
   a natural fit — LLM calls are I/O-bound. SQLite via aiosqlite keeps things
  simple. SSE integrates cleanly with HTMX via hx-sse.

  Cons: If multiple LLMs are generating simultaneously for auto-responses, they
  compete for the event loop (though asyncio handles I/O concurrency well). Long
   auto-responses block a server thread.

  Approach B: FastAPI + Background Worker Queue

  FastAPI handles HTTP and SSE. LLM generation is offloaded to a background
  worker (arq + Redis). Frontend receives streaming via the worker pushing to
  Redis pub/sub, bridged to SSE.

  Pros: Robust for long-running multi-LLM scenarios. Workers can be scaled
  independently. Graceful handling of crashes/timeouts.

  Cons: Requires Redis. More moving parts (worker process, queue, broker).
  Operational complexity is overkill for a single-user tool. Debugging across
  process boundaries is harder.

  Approach C: Simple Polling (no streaming)

  LLM calls run in background threads/tasks. Frontend polls for new messages
  every few seconds. Full messages appear when ready — no streaming at all.

  Pros: Simplest possible backend. No SSE/WebSocket state to manage. Easy to
  reason about and debug.

  Cons: No streaming (contradicts our hybrid delivery model). Polling is
  wasteful. Feels sluggish compared to real-time updates.

  ---
  My recommendation is Approach A. It delivers the hybrid streaming model
  without extra infrastructure, and asyncio is well-suited to I/O-bound LLM
  calls. Worth noting: HTMX SSE support is solid and well-documented.

  Does Approach A sound right to you, or would you prefer one of the others?

● Data model is on screen at http://localhost:63257. Four tables:

  - conversations — a group chat session
  - messages — each message links to a conversation and optionally to an LLM
  config (null for user messages)
  - llm_configs — each LLM participant: name, provider, model, participation
  mode, system prompt
  - api_keys — encrypted at rest with Fernet, shared across configs (e.g., two
  OpenAI models → one key)

  Key subtlety: LLM configs aren't "bound" to a conversation in a join table.
  They're referenced per-message, so you can swap configs or change settings
  without breaking history.

  Does the data model look right?

  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Route: POST /fragments/llm-configs
  Returns: Create/edit LLM config
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Route: DELETE /fragments/llm-configs/{id}
  Returns: Remove an LLM config

  SSE Streaming

  ┌────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │           Route            │                  Returns                   │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                            │ SSE stream — subscribes to token-by-token  │
  │ GET                        │ updates for messages currently being       │
  │ /stream/conversations/{id} │ generated. Events: chunk (token), done     │
  │                            │ (message complete), error                  │
  └────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Config / Keys

  ┌────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │           Route            │               Returns                │
  ├────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ GET /settings              │ Settings page (API keys, app config) │
  ├────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ POST /settings/keys        │ Add/update API key                   │
  ├────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ DELETE /settings/keys/{id} │ Remove API key                       │
  └────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘

  Orchestration Flow

  When a user posts a message:
  1. Save user's message to DB
  2. Determine which LLMs should respond (check configs: @mentioned, always-on,
  or random roll for probabilistic)
  3. Fire concurrent async tasks for each responding LLM
  4. Each task: build prompt from full history → call provider API → stream
  chunks to SSE queue → save completed message to DB
  5. Return HTML fragment: user's message + "generating..." placeholders for
  each responding LLM

  The SSE endpoint lets the frontend swap those placeholders for streaming text
  in real time.

  ---

● Makes sense — in a group chat with multiple LLMs, unconstrained responses
  would flood the conversation. I'll add max_response_chars to llm_configs with
  a 400-char default.

  ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  - A 400-char cap keeps the conversation fast-paced and readable — multiple
  LLMs × long responses = walls of text.
  - Making it a configurable per-LLM field (not a hardcoded constant) means
  users can let one "deep thinker" LLM go longer while keeping others terse.
  - The cap is applied at the orchestrator level (in the prompt: "keep your
  response under N characters") rather than truncating output, so responses stay
   coherent.
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

● Orchestration flow is up at http://localhost:63257. Key design points:

  - Three trigger modes: @mention (always fires), always-on, and probabilistic
  (dice roll against configured probability)
  - LLM-to-LLM chains: LLMs can @mention each other, triggering follow-up
  responses. Capped at depth 2 to prevent infinite chatter.
  - Safeguards: self-reply prevention, max 5 concurrent API calls, 60s timeout
  per call

  ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  - The depth limit of 2 is important — without it, two "always-on" LLMs would
  ping-pong forever. Each message triggers the other, creating an infinite loop
  that burns API credits.
  - Self-reply prevention is the other half of the same guard: an LLM that just
  spoke won't respond to its own message even in "always" mode, unless
  explicitly @mentioned.
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

● SSE Streaming Flow

  Here's how streaming works end-to-end for a single LLM response:

  User sends message
         │
         ▼
  POST /fragments/conversations/{id}/messages
    → Returns HTML: user message bubble + "generating..." placeholder
    → HX-Trigger response header: "sse:connect" to /stream/conversations/{id}
         │
         ▼
  HTMX connects to SSE endpoint
    GET /stream/conversations/{id}
    (Server keeps connection open)
         │
         ▼
  Asyncio task pushes events to an asyncio.Queue
         │
         ▼
  SSE endpoint reads from queue, yields events:
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │ event: chunk                                  │
    │ data: {"llm_config_id": 3, "token": "You"}    │
    │                                               │
    │ event: chunk                                  │
    │ data: {"llm_config_id": 3, "token": " are"}   │
    │                                               │
    │ event: chunk                                  │
    │ data: {"llm_config_id": 1, "token": "Also"}   │  ← interleaved!
    │                                               │
    │ event: done                                   │
    │ data: {"llm_config_id": 3, "message_id": 42}  │
    │                                               │
    │ event: error                                  │
    │ data: {"llm_config_id": 2, "error": "timeout"}│
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         │
         ▼
  HTMX hx-sse extension processes each event:
    • "chunk" → append token to the matching placeholder's text content
    • "done"  → swap placeholder for final message bubble with timestamp
    • "error" → swap placeholder for error indicator + retry link

  Key details:
  - One SSE connection per conversation — all active LLM streams share it.
  Events are tagged with llm_config_id so the frontend routes tokens to the
  right placeholder.
  - Interleaved tokens — when two LLMs stream simultaneously, tokens arrive
  interleaved. The frontend handles this naturally since each chunk identifies
  its source.
  - asyncio.Queue as the fan-in point — each LLM task pushes to a shared queue.
  The SSE endpoint reads from it. This keeps the provider adapters decoupled
  from the transport.
  - Connection lifecycle — SSE stays open as long as any LLM is generating.
  Auto-closes after all tasks complete + 5 second quiet period.

  ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  - Using a single SSE connection per conversation (not per LLM) is deliberate.
  Multiple SSE connections would hit browser connection limits (6 per domain).
  Tagging events with llm_config_id lets one stream serve N concurrent LLMs.
  - asyncio.Queue is the right fan-in primitive here — it's thread-safe,
  awaitable, and built into the standard library. No need for Redis pub/sub when
   everything runs in one process.
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Does the streaming approach look right?

● Error Handling Strategy

  Here's how failures are handled at each layer:

  ┌─────────────┬───────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │    Layer    │    Failure    │                 Handling                 │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Provider    │ Timeout (60s) │ Placeholder replaced with "X timed out"  │
  │ API         │               │ + retry link. Other LLMs unaffected.     │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Provider    │ Rate limit    │ Exponential backoff, up to 3 retries. If │
  │ API         │ (429)         │  all fail, show rate-limit error.        │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Provider    │ Invalid API   │ Bubble up to UI immediately — "Check     │
  │ API         │ key (401)     │ your API key for [provider]." No retry.  │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Provider    │ Server error  │ One retry after 2s. If still failing,    │
  │ API         │ (5xx)         │ show error + retry link.                 │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ SSE         │ Client        │ Tasks continue generating. Messages      │
  │ connection  │ disconnects   │ still saved to DB. On reconnect, missed  │
  │             │               │ messages load from DB.                   │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ SSE         │ Server-side   │ HTMX hx-sse auto-reconnects. On          │
  │ connection  │ drop          │ reconnect, server replays messages       │
  │             │               │ completed since disconnect.              │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │             │               │ Log error, show "message not saved"      │
  │ Database    │ Write failure │ warning. Conversation still readable     │
  │             │               │ in-memory for current session.           │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │             │ History too   │ Orchestrator trims oldest messages       │
  │ Token limit │ long          │ (excluding @mentions) to fit context     │
  │             │               │ window. Logs a warning.                  │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ LLM-to-LLM  │               │ Silently stop. The @mention is still     │
  │ chain       │ Depth > 2     │ visible in chat, user can manually       │
  │             │               │ follow up.                               │
  └─────────────┴───────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘

  User-facing error states (what shows in chat):
  - "Timed out" — greyed-out bubble with a "retry" link
  - "Check API key" — amber warning with a link to settings
  - "Rate limited — retrying..." — briefly shown, then either resolves or shows
  "Failed after 3 attempts"
  - "Stream interrupted" — shown when SSE drops, auto-resolves on reconnect

  Design principle: one LLM's failure shouldn't affect others. If Claude times
  out and GPT responds fine, the user still gets GPT's answer. Errors are scoped
   to individual LLM participants.

  ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  - The key principle is fault isolation — each LLM is an independent async task
   with its own error handler. If one provider is down, the others keep working.
   This is the main reason the orchestrator spawns separate tasks rather than
  batching LLM calls.
  - Non-retryable errors (401 invalid key, 400 bad request) fail immediately
  without backoff. Only transient errors (429, 5xx) get retries. This prevents
  wasting time on permanent failures.
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

● Testing Strategy

  ┌──────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────┐
  │  Test Type   │                Scope                 │     Tooling      │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │              │ Provider adapters (mocked API        │                  │
  │ Unit tests   │ responses), participation logic      │ pytest +         │
  │              │ (trigger rules), token counting,     │ pytest-asyncio   │
  │              │ prompt building                      │                  │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │              │ Database operations, SSE queue       │                  │
  │ Integration  │ fan-in, full message → response flow │ pytest + httpx   │
  │ tests        │  with mocked LLM calls, API routes   │ AsyncClient      │
  │              │ return correct HTML fragments        │                  │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Manual /     │ Real LLM calls (spot-check with      │                  │
  │ smoke tests  │ actual API keys), streaming UX in    │ Browser          │
  │              │ browser, @mention autocomplete       │                  │
  └──────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────┘

  Key test scenarios:

  - Participation logic: "always" responds to every message, "mention_only"
  skips unless @mentioned, "probabilistic" rolls correctly at boundaries (0.0
  never, 1.0 always), @mention overrides all modes
  - Orchestrator: multiple concurrent LLM tasks all complete, one task failure
  doesn't block others, depth limit stops infinite chains, self-reply is
  prevented
  - SSE fan-in: tokens from 2+ concurrent streams are correctly tagged with
  llm_config_id, done/error events fire correctly
  - Context window: history trimming respects @mention preservation, token
  counting is accurate
  - Database: CRUD for conversations/messages/configs/keys, cascade delete
  (conversation → messages), key encryption round-trip

  ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  - The provider adapters are mocked in unit tests, not the real APIs. This is
  obvious but important: the adapter boundary is the seam that makes everything
  testable without real API keys or network calls.
  - The participation logic is pure functions (no I/O), so they're the fastest
  and most reliable tests in the suite. Test these exhaustively — they're the
  core business logic.
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  Project Structure:

  agent-chat/
  ├── app/
  │   ├── main.py              # FastAPI app, lifespan, route registration
  │   ├── routes/
  │   │   ├── pages.py         # Full page routes (GET /)
  │   │   ├── fragments.py     # HTMX fragment routes
  │   │   ├── stream.py        # SSE endpoint
  │   │   └── settings.py      # Settings/API key management
  │   ├── orchestrator.py      # Multi-LLM coordination logic
  │   ├── providers/
  │   │   ├── base.py          # Abstract adapter interface
  │   │   ├── openai.py
  │   │   ├── anthropic.py
  │   │   └── google.py
  │   ├── db/
  │   │   ├── models.py        # SQLAlchemy or dataclass models
  │   │   ├── queries.py       # Database operations
  │   │   └── migrations.py    # Schema setup/migration
  │   ├── templates/
  │   │   ├── base.html        # Shell layout
  │   │   ├── fragments/       # HTMX partials
  │   │   └── pages/           # Full page templates
  │   └── crypto.py            # Fernet key encryption
  ├── tests/
  │   ├── test_participation.py
  │   ├── test_orchestrator.py
  │   ├── test_providers.py
  │   ├── test_routes.py
  │   └── test_db.py
  ├── static/                   # Minimal CSS/JS (if any)
  ├── requirements.txt
  └── README.md

  That covers the full design. Here's a quick recap of everything we've settled:

  ┌────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │      Area      │                Decision                 │
  ├────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Stack          │ Python FastAPI + HTMX + SQLite          │
  ├────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Layout         │ Two-panel (sidebar + chat)              │
  ├────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ LLM trigger    │ @mention / always / probabilistic       │
  ├────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ LLM chains     │ Yes, depth-limited to 2                 │
  ├────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Streaming      │ Single SSE connection, async fan-in     │
  ├────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Keys           │ BYO, Fernet-encrypted in SQLite         │
  ├────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Users          │ Single-user, no auth                    │
  ├────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Response cap   │ 400 chars default, per-LLM configurable │
  ├────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Error handling │ Fault-isolated per LLM                  │
  └────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────┘

● Self-review found two gaps against the spec. Let me fix them.

  Gap 1: Spec requires exponential backoff retry (up to 3) for rate limits and
  one retry for 5xx errors — the orchestrator doesn't implement this yet.

  Gap 2: Token-by-token SSE streaming needs a small JS handler — HTMX's built-in
   hx-sse handles element swaps but can't append individual tokens to specific
  placeholders.

● 26 tests pass, pushed to GitHub.

  ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  - The form used <input type="number"> for api_key_id, expecting users to know
  the auto-incremented DB ID of their API key. A dropdown (<select>) populated
  from the api_keys table is both more usable and prevents errors — the backend
  gets the correct integer ID without the user ever seeing it.
  - The {% if api_keys %} guard with a link to Settings when no keys exist
  prevents the form from silently failing. Without it, users would see an empty
  dropdown with no explanation.
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

  The LLM config form now shows a dropdown of your saved API keys (e.g., "openai
   — ••••••••") instead of asking for a numeric ID. If no keys are configured
  yet, it links you to Settings to add one first.

● 26 tests pass, pushed. The fix:

  - Before: The "保存" button was always shown, but the API key dropdown only
  rendered when keys existed. No keys → missing required field → 422.
  - After: The entire form (including the save button) is inside {% if api_keys 
  %}. No keys → a message with a link to Settings appears instead. Keys exist →
  full form with the dropdown.

  ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  - FastAPI's 422 means "your JSON/form fields didn't match the endpoint's
  expected parameters." The quickest way to debug these: check the browser's
  Network tab to see exactly which fields were sent in the request body, then
  compare against the function signature's Form(...) parameters.
  - HTMX form submissions include all form fields within the <form> that
  triggered the request. If a field is conditionally excluded ({% if %}), the
  field simply won't be in the POST data — and if the endpoint requires it, you
  get 422.
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

● The link should navigate to /settings, but let me just explain the full
  workflow:

  Steps to add an API key and LLM:

  1. Click the gear icon (⚙) in the top-right of any chat header to open
  Settings
  2. In the "API 密钥" section, select your provider (e.g., "openai"), paste
  your key (e.g., sk-abc123...), and click "添加密钥"
  3. After adding, scroll down to "LLM 配置" — the key you just added will now
  appear in the dropdown
  4. Fill in the LLM form: name, provider, model (e.g., gpt-4o), participation
  mode, and select your key
  5. Click "保存"

  The "Add one in Settings" link goes to the Settings page where step 2 happens,
   but you need to go there first. Here's the direct path:
  http://127.0.0.1:8000/settings

  Let me fix two things to make this smoother: a quick-add key form directly
  inline so you don't need to leave the LLM config panel, and better feedback
  when the link is clicked.

● Found the issues. There are three bugs:

  1. SSE "error" event collides with the browser's native EventSource error
  event — our custom error events are swallowed
  2. Silent exception swallowing in the retry block — if a retry fails, no error
   is pushed, so "generating..." spins forever
  3. provider may be unbound in the except block if the error happens before
  assignment

  Let me fix all three.

● DeepSeek works fine. But I found the real issue — DeepSeek uses reasoning 
  tokens that consume from max_tokens. Here's the proof:

  completion_tokens: 42  (39 reasoning + 3 content = "hello world")

  Your configs have max_response_chars=400, which becomes max_tokens=400.
  DeepSeek burns most of those on internal reasoning, leaving nothing for the
  visible response. Let me fix this.

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)
  ⎿  PreToolUse:Read hook error                 ⎿  Failed with non-blocking status code: No
  ⎿  PostToolUse:Read hook error  ⎿  Failed with non-blocking status code: No stderr output

   value) to the APIs, but every provider expects "assistant". A one-line
  mapping {"user": "user", "llm": "assistant", "system": "system"} fixes it.

  Restart the server and try again — all four LLMs should respond now.

● The key fix: the SSE connection script is now embedded directly in the HTML 
  response as a <script> tag. Previously it relied on detecting HX-Trigger: 
  sseConnect via htmx:afterOnLoad, which has multiple failure points (header
  accessibility, event timing, DOM availability).

  When HTMX appends the response to the chat, the browser immediately executes
  the script and connects to the SSE stream. No header detection needed.

  The console will also show these new trace logs:
  [SSE] Connection opened for conversation 1        ← frontend connected
  [SSE] Sending token for llm_config_id=4            ← token flowing
  [LLM] DeepSeek V4 Flash: stream complete, 87 chars ← API returned content
  [LLM] DeepSeek V4 Flash: complete event pushed     ← saved to DB + queued

  Restart the server and try a message. You should see the full trace, and the
  "generating..." placeholders should update in real time.

       85 +function handleMentionInput(input) {                          
       86 +    const cursorPos = input.selectionStart;                   
       87 +    const textBeforeCursor = input.value.substring(0, cursorPo
          +s);                                                           
       88 +    const atMatch = textBeforeCursor.match(/@(\S*)$/);        
       89 +    const dropdown = document.getElementById('mention-dropdown
          +');                                                           
       90 +                                                              
       91 +    if (atMatch) {                                            
       92 +        const query = atMatch[1].toLowerCase();               
       93 +        const matches = LLM_NAMES.filter(n => n.toLowerCase().
          +includes(query));                                             
       94 +        if (matches.length > 0) {                             
       95 +            dropdown.innerHTML = matches.map((n, i) =>        
       96 +                '<div class="mention-item" data-index="' + i +
          + '" data-name="' + n + '"' +                                  
       97 +                ' style="padding: 6px 12px; font-size: 12px; c
          +ursor: pointer; color: var(--text-primary);"' +

       99 +                '>' + n + '</div>'                            
      100 +            ).join('');                                       
      101 +            dropdown.style.display = 'block';                 
      102 +            dropdown._selectedIndex = -1;                     
      103 +            dropdown._matches = matches;                      
      104 +            return;                                           
      105 +        }                                                     
      106 +    }                                                         
      107 +    dropdown.style.display = 'none';                          
      108 +    dropdown._matches = [];                                   
      109 +}                                                             
      110 +                                                              
      111 +function handleMentionKeydown(e, input) {                     
      112 +    const dropdown = document.getElementById('mention-dropdown
          +');                                                           
      113 +    if (dropdown.style.display === 'none') return;            
      114 +    if (!dropdown._matches) return;                           
      115 +                                                              
      116 +    if (e.key === 'ArrowDown') {                              
      117 +        e.preventDefault();                                   
      118 +        dropdown._selectedIndex = Math.min(dropdown._selectedI
          +ndex + 1, dropdown._matches.length - 1);                      
      119 +        updateMentionSelection(dropdown);                     
      120 +    } else if (e.key === 'ArrowUp') {                         
      121 +        e.preventDefault();                                   
      122 +        dropdown._selectedIndex = Math.max(dropdown._selectedI
          +ndex - 1, -1);                                                
      123 +        updateMentionSelection(dropdown);                     
      124 +    } else if (e.key === 'Enter' || e.key === 'Tab') {        
      125 +        if (dropdown._selectedIndex >= 0) {                   
      126 +            e.preventDefault();                               
      127 +            insertMention(input, dropdown._matches[dropdown._s
          +electedIndex]);                                               
      128 +        }                                                     
      129 +    } else if (e.key === 'Escape') {                          
      130 +        dropdown.style.display = 'none';                      
      131 +    }                                                         
      132 +}                                                             
      133 +                                                              
      134 +function updateMentionSelection(dropdown) {                   
      135 +    const items = dropdown.querySelectorAll('.mention-item'); 
      136 +    items.forEach((item, i) => {                              
      137 +        item.style.background = i === dropdown._selectedIndex 
          +? 'var(--border-color)' : '';                                 
      138 +    });                                                       
      139 +}

      144 +}                                                             
      145 +                                                              
      146 +function insertMention(input, name) {                         
      147 +    const cursorPos = input.selectionStart;                   
      148 +    const textBefore = input.value.substring(0, cursorPos);   
      149 +    const textAfter = input.value.substring(cursorPos);       
      150 +    const atPos = textBefore.lastIndexOf('@');                
      151 +    const newText = textBefore.substring(0, atPos) + '@' + nam
          +e + ' ' + textAfter;                                          
      152 +    input.value = newText;                                    
      153 +    input.focus();                                            
      154 +    const newCursor = atPos + name.length + 2;                
      155 +    input.setSelectionRange(newCursor, newCursor);            
      156 +    document.getElementById('mention-dropdown').style.display 
          += 'none';                                                     
      157 +}

      14 +async def _auto_title(conversation_id: int, first_message: str,
         + config):                                                      
      15 +    """Generate a short title for the conversation using the fi
         +rst available LLM."""                                          
      16 +    try:                                                       
      17 +        from app.crypto import decrypt                         
      18 +        from app.providers import get_provider                 
      19 +        from app.providers.base import LLMRequest              
      20 +                                                               
      21 +        key_record = await queries.get_api_key(config.api_key_i
         +d)                                                             
      22 +        if not key_record:                                     
      23 +            return                                             
      24 +        api_key = decrypt(key_record.key_encrypted)            
      25 +        provider = get_provider(config.provider)               
      26 +                                                               
      27 +        request = LLMRequest(                                  
      28 +            model=config.model,                                
      29 +            api_key=api_key,                                   
      30 +            system_prompt="Generate a SHORT title (max 6 words)
         + summarizing the user's message. Reply with ONLY the title, no 
         +quotes.",                                                      
      31 +            messages=[{"role": "user", "content": first_message
         +}],                                                            
      32 +            max_tokens=30,                                     
      33 +        )                                                      
      34 +        response = await provider.generate(request)            
      35 +        title = response.content.strip().strip('"').strip("'")[
         +:50]                                                           
      36 +        if title:                                              
      37 +            await queries.update_conversation_title(conversatio
         +n_id, title)                                                   
      38 +            print(f"[AutoTitle] Conversation {conversation_id}:
         + {title}", flush=True)                                         
      39 +    except Exception as e:                                     
      40 +        print(f"[AutoTitle] Failed: {e}", flush=True)          
      41 +                                                               
      42 +

● 26 tests pass, pushed. Here's what each improvement does:

  #1 — Auto-title generation: After your first message in a new conversation,
  the first available LLM automatically generates a short title (max 6 words).
  The title appears in the sidebar. New conversations won't say "Untitled"
  anymore.

  #2 — Clickable LLM list: Click the "4个LLM在线" status text in the chat header
   — a dropdown shows each LLM's name, avatar, and provider. Click anywhere
  outside to dismiss.

  #3 — @mention autocomplete: Type @ in the message box and start typing a name
  — a dropdown filters matching LLMs. Use arrow keys to navigate, Enter/Tab to
  select, or click with mouse. The selected name is inserted as @Name .

  ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  - The auto-title uses the first LLM in your config list and calls generate()
  (non-streaming) since we only need a few words. It runs as a background
  asyncio.create_task so it doesn't delay the message response.
  - The @mention autocomplete is pure vanilla JS — no library needed.
  selectionStart, lastIndexOf('@'), and setSelectionRange handle cursor-aware
  text insertion around the @ trigger.
  - The LLM count dropdown is pure CSS toggle — classList.toggle('show') plus a
  document-level click listener to close it. The LLM name data is injected as a
  JSON array via Jinja2's | tojson filter.
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────

● Two fixes pushed:

  #2 — Merged answers: Placeholder IDs were placeholder-4, reused for every
  message from LLM #4. getElementById returned the FIRST one (from a previous
  message), so new tokens appended to old bubbles. Now IDs are placeholder-4-15
  (unique per user message), e.g., placeholder-4-15, placeholder-4-16.

  #1 — Auto-title: Added a diagnostic log. Restart, create a new conversation,
  send a message. You'll see:
  [AutoTitle] Check: user_count=1, configs=4
  [AutoTitle] Triggering for conversation 5 with LLM Deepseek V4 Pro
  Or if it's not triggering, the user_count or configs values will tell us
  exactly why.

  title generation; other providers keep 30.

  message.

● Pushed. Three changes:

  Title edit: Click the ✎ icon next to the title to edit inline. Press Enter to
  save, Escape to cancel, or click away. Once you edit a title manually,
  auto-title won't overwrite it (checks title_empty before generating).

  SSE race fix: window.__sseConnection now tracks the active EventSource. Before
   creating a new one, the old one is closed — preventing old connections from
  stealing events meant for new placeholders.

  Merged answers: Placeholder IDs remain unique per message (placeholder-4-15).
  Combined with the SSE close fix above, this should prevent cross-message
  content merging.

● Pushed. Title editing now works from both locations:

  - Header ✎ — inline edit the top title. On save, the sidebar auto-refreshes
  via HTMX.
  - Sidebar ✎ — each conversation has its own ✎ icon. On save, the header title
  updates too (via OOB swap in the response).

  The PUT route returns both elements in one response:
  <span id="conv-title-3">New Title</span>
  <span id="conversation-title-text" hx-swap-oob="true">New Title</span>

  HTMX processes the main target (sidebar) and the hx-swap-oob (header) in a
  single response.

● Done. LLM responses now render with full Markdown support:

  What's rendered:
  - Bold, italic, inline code
  - Code blocks with syntax-friendly dark background
  - Bullet lists and numbered lists
  - Blockquotes with a left border
  - Tables, links, horizontal rules

  How it works:
  - marked.js parses Markdown to HTML on the client side
  - Streaming: tokens accumulate as plain text, then on complete the bubble is
  rendered as HTML via marked.parse()
  - Existing messages: a script at page load converts all .message-bubble
  content to HTML
  - The .rendered class prevents re-rendering already-formatted bubbles

● 28 tests pass, pushed. Here's the summary:

  #1 — @Everyone mention: Type @ and select "Everyone" or "所有AI" from the
  dropdown. All LLMs respond regardless of their participation mode. Also works
  with @everyone, @所有人, @all. The @Everyone mention bypasses mention_only
  restrictions and even the self-reply lock.

  #2 — Default 200 char limit: New LLM configs default to 200 characters (down
  from 400). Your existing configs were updated too. If you want more detailed
  responses, you can increase max_response_chars per LLM in Settings.

● Pushed. Two new features:

  Edit LLM Configs: Each config card now has an ✎ Edit button. Click to reveal
  an inline form pre-filled with current values — you can change Name, Model,
  Participation Mode, and API Key. Save updates in place; Cancel reverts.

  Delete Conversations: Each conversation in the sidebar now has a ✕ delete
  icon. Click it, confirm the dialog, and the conversation plus all its messages
   are removed. If you're viewing that conversation when deleted, the page
  redirects to home.

● Fixed. Two layers of protection:

  1. Don't save empty responses — if an LLM returns zero content (e.g., DeepSeek
   reasoning eats all tokens), it shows as an error instead of saving an empty
  message
  2. Filter empty messages from context — _build_messages now skips any message
  with blank content, so one bad message can't poison future requests

  The 3 empty messages already in your DB were cleaned up. Kimi should work now.

        6 -## Overview                                                   
        5 +## Overview / 概述                                            
        6  
        7  A web-based chat application where a user converses with multi
           ple AI LLMs in a single group-chat conversation. LLMs can talk
            to each other — they can @mention one another, respond autono
           mously based on configurable participation modes, and build on
            each other's responses.
        8  
       10 -## Core Decisions                                             
        9 +一个基于 Web 的聊天应用，用户可以在一个群聊对话中与多个 AI LLM
          + 交流。LLM 之间可以互相交谈 — 它们可以 @提及彼此，根据可配置的
          +参与模式自主回复，并在彼此的回复基础上继续讨论。

       13 -|------|----------|                                           
       14 -| Interaction model | Group chat — user + LLMs as equal partic
          -ipants |                                                      
       15 -| LLM participation | Configurable per LLM: @mention, always-o
          -n, probabilistic |                                            
       16 -| LLM connectivity | User brings own API keys (BYO) |         
       17 -| Deployment model | SPA + thin backend proxy (avoids CORS) | 
       18 -| Tech stack | Python FastAPI + HTMX + SQLite |               
       19 -| Message delivery | Hybrid: SSE streaming for direct interact
          -ions, full-message for auto-responses |                       
       20 -| LLM context | Full conversation history per LLM |           
       21 -| Persistence | Conversations persist across sessions |       
       22 -| Users | Single-user, no auth |                              
       23 -| Response length | 400 characters default, configurable per L
          -LM |                                                          
       24 -| Language | Simplified Chinese default, English optional, swi
          -tchable in sidebar + settings |

       28 -Single FastAPI process using asyncio. No Redis, no background 
          -workers. LLM calls are I/O-bound, so `asyncio.gather` handles 
          -concurrency.

      156 -## Multi-LLM Orchestration                                    
      182 +### SSE / 流式                                                
      183 +| Route | Description |                                       
      184 +|-------|-------------|                                       
      185 +| `GET /stream/conversations/{id}` | SSE event stream / SSE 事
          +件流 |                                                        
      186  
      158 -### Participation Trigger Logic                               
      159 -                                                              
      160 -When a user or LLM sends a message:                           
      161 -                                                              
      162 -1. **Parse @mentions** — extract mentioned LLM names from mess
          -age text                                                      
      163 -2. **For each configured LLM**, determine if it should respond
          -:                                                             
      164 -   - `@mentioned` → always respond (bypasses all other rules) 
      165 -   - `participation_mode = "always"` → respond to every messag
          -e                                                             
      166 -   - `participation_mode = "probabilistic"` → roll `random() <
          - probability`                                                 
      167 -   - `participation_mode = "mention_only"` → skip unless @ment
          -ioned                                                         
      168 -3. **Launch concurrent async tasks** for each responding LLM  
      169 -4. **Chain detection**: if an LLM @mentions another LLM, step 
          -2 runs again for the new message (depth-limited)              
      170 -                                                              
      171 -### Safeguards                                                
      172 -                                                              
      173 -- **Depth limit**: LLM-to-LLM chains capped at 2 hops         
      174 -- **Self-reply prevention**: an LLM won't respond to its own m
          -essage (even in "always" mode) unless @mentioned              
      175 -- **Concurrency cap**: max 5 simultaneous LLM API calls; exces
          -s queue                                                       
      176 -- **Timeout**: 60s per LLM call                               
      177 -                                                              
      178 -## SSE Streaming Flow                                         
      179 -                                                              
      180 -One SSE connection per conversation. Events are tagged with `l
          -lm_config_id` so the frontend routes tokens to the correct pla
          -ceholder.                                                     
      181 -                                                              
      182 -```                                                           
      183 -POST /fragments/conversations/{id}/messages                   
      184 -  → Returns HTML: user message + placeholders                 
      185 -  → HX-Trigger: connect SSE                                   
      186 -                                                              
      187 -GET /stream/conversations/{id}                                
      188 -  → event: chunk  {"llm_config_id": 3, "token": "..."}        
      189 -  → event: done   {"llm_config_id": 3, "message_id": 42}      
      190 -  → event: error  {"llm_config_id": 2, "error": "timeout"}    
      191 -```                                                           
      192 -                                                              
      193 -All LLM tasks push to a shared `asyncio.Queue`. The SSE endpoi
          -nt reads from it and yields Server-Sent Events.               
      194 -                                                              
      195 -## Error Handling                                             
      196 -                                                              
      197 -| Failure | Handling |                                        
      198 -|---------|----------|                                        
      199 -| Timeout (60s) | Placeholder → "timed out" + retry link |    
      200 -| Rate limit (429) | Exponential backoff, up to 3 retries |   
      201 -| Invalid key (401) | Immediate error, link to settings |     
      202 -| Server error (5xx) | One retry, then error + retry link |   
      203 -| SSE disconnect | Tasks continue, missed messages loaded from
          - DB on reconnect |                                            
      204 -| DB write failure | Log, warn, conversation readable in-memor
          -y |                                                           
      205 -| Token limit exceeded | Trim oldest messages (preserving @men
          -tions) |                                                      
      206 -                                                              
      207 -**Fault isolation**: one LLM's failure never blocks another. E
          -rrors are scoped to individual participants.                  
      208 -

          -page

          -tatus/error messages. LLM responses and user messages are neve
          -r translated.

          -language stored in `settings` table + cookie

     API Documentation Generator Plugin — Implementation Plan

     Context

     The agent-chat project has 18 API endpoints across 4 route files, 5 data
     models, and 6 LLM providers — but no API documentation. We need a reusable
     Claude Code plugin that generates comprehensive API docs through a
     multi-agent pipeline: analyze endpoints, write descriptions, generate usage
      examples, and audit for completeness.

     Plugin Directory Structure

     api-docs-generator/
     ├── .claude-plugin/
     │   └── plugin.json
     ├── agents/
     │   ├── endpoint-analyzer.md
     │   ├── description-writer.md
     │   ├── example-generator.md
     │   └── completeness-reviewer.md
     ├── skills/
     │   └── generate-api-docs/
     │       ├── SKILL.md
     │       └── references/
     │           └── output-format.md
     └── README.md

     Implementation Steps

     Phase 1: Plugin Scaffold

     Create directory structure, plugin.json manifest, and README.

     Phase 2: Agent Files

     Write 4 agent .md files with YAML frontmatter and complete system prompts:

     ┌───────────────────────┬────────┬─────────┬─────────┬────────────────────┐
     │         Agent         │ Model  │  Color  │  Tools  │        Role        │
     ├───────────────────────┼────────┼─────────┼─────────┼────────────────────┤
     │                       │        │         │ Read,   │ Scan routes →      │
     │ endpoint-analyzer     │ haiku  │ cyan    │ Grep,   │ endpoint inventory │
     │                       │        │         │ Glob    │  table             │
     ├───────────────────────┼────────┼─────────┼─────────┼────────────────────┤
     │                       │        │         │ Read,   │ Write detailed     │
     │ description-writer    │ sonnet │ green   │ Write   │ per-endpoint       │
     │                       │        │         │         │ descriptions       │
     ├───────────────────────┼────────┼─────────┼─────────┼────────────────────┤
     │ example-generator     │ sonnet │ magenta │ Read,   │ Generate curl      │
     │                       │        │         │ Write   │ usage examples     │
     ├───────────────────────┼────────┼─────────┼─────────┼────────────────────┤
     │                       │        │         │ Read,   │ Audit docs vs      │
     │ completeness-reviewer │ haiku  │ red     │ Grep,   │ source → review    │
     │                       │        │         │ Glob    │ report             │
     └───────────────────────┴────────┴─────────┴─────────┴────────────────────┘

     Phase 3: Skill File

     Write skills/generate-api-docs/SKILL.md — the 4-phase pipeline
     orchestrator:
     1. Dispatch endpoint-analyzer → inventory
     2. Dispatch description-writer → docs/api/*.md
     3. Dispatch example-generator → examples + inline snippets
     4. Dispatch completeness-reviewer → review-report.md

     Phase 4: Reference File

     Write references/output-format.md — Markdown templates for all 8 output
     files.

     Phase 5: Install and Run

     Install the plugin and invoke /generate-api-docs on the agent-chat project
     to produce docs/api/.

     Output Files Generated

     docs/api/
     ├── index.md          # Endpoint summary table + router map
     ├── pages.md          # GET /, /conversations/{id}, /settings
     ├── fragments.md      # 12 HTMX fragment endpoints
     ├── streaming.md      # SSE protocol (token/complete/llm-error events)
     ├── settings.md       # POST/DELETE /settings/keys
     ├── models.md         # 5 dataclasses + 7-provider registry
     ├── examples.md       # curl commands for all 18 endpoints
     └── review-report.md  # Errors/Warnings/Info

     Verification

     1. Install plugin: copy to ~/.claude/plugins/api-docs-generator/
     2. Reload plugins
     3. Run 生成 API 文档 or /generate-api-docs from the agent-chat project
     4. Verify all 8 files created under docs/api/
     5. Spot-check: compare documented parameters against source code for 3
     endpoints
     6. Run python -m pytest tests/ -q to ensure no regressions

  ★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────
  "Brutalist Terminal" design system:

  - Typography: IBM Plex Mono structures the UI chrome (sidebar, headers,
  buttons, inputs, code) with uppercase tracking — it feels like a precision
  instrument. Crimson Pro warms the message content with editorial serif warmth
  — the human voice in the machine. This pairing avoids the generic AI-chat look
   entirely.
  - Amber + Teal accents: Amber (#f59e0b) is the primary action color — send
  buttons, active items, focus rings, markdown headings. Teal (#14b8a6) is the
  secondary — links, inline code. Rose (#fb7185) for errors. Three distinct
  accent hues create visual hierarchy without clutter.
  - Atmosphere: A noise-texture SVG overlay at 3% opacity breaks the sterile
  flat-dark look. Two radial gradients create subtle amber/teal vignettes at the
   edges of the main content area. Borders are luminous (rgba white 2-8%)
  instead of invisible solid colors.
  - Motion: Message stagger-in (@keyframes messageIn), generating pulse
  (@keyframes breathe), dropdown slide-in (@keyframes dropdownIn), and 150ms
  hover transitions on everything. All CSS-only — no JS animation libraries.
  - Gradient avatars: Each LLM gets a unique 135° gradient instead of flat
  colors. Rounded to 6px (squircle) instead of perfect circles — a subtle
  brutalist touch.
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────
